In like Flynn!
#16075 1 2007-07-05T22:04:18Z gloomp
Okay, I know what you're thinking, "oh no, another noob map pack." But this, however, has not, like a noob level pack, been rushed. Nor is it something that is so small it could hardly be called a pack. No, it is a full column of levels, plus a few bonus levels, just for kicks. I've been working on this for weeks, but keep in mind when I started it I had just started to grasp how to make a good level.

I call it A Beginning because I think of it as the start of my mapmaking career.

Also, this is my first try at this, so please, be gentle.

p.s. I'd just like to mention I've read the forum rules, Jeff Killian's (sorry if I spelled that wrong) guide to mapmaking, and a lot of the stupid posts noobs make.

Without further ado, the pack!

#345020 2 2007-07-19T13:55:19Z gloomp
Once you've made your map, copy the data from the upper text box, then put the data in userlevels format which goes like this:

$Map Name#Author Name#Map Type#Data That You Copied From Upper Text Box#

After you've done that, go to the folder where you keep n and all the documents that came with it, and open "userlevels.txt" and paste the data in there beneath the clearly marked line.
Now just save the changes, and your done!

p.s. Please refrain from calling it "n game" in the future. Just call it "n"

#350774 49 2007-08-03T15:25:09Z gloomp
Some ideas off the top of my head:


Objects Only. Basically means no tiles except for the edge tiles.
Cave Style. Ninja spawns on the surface, but quickly goes down a hole, where most of the gameplay happens. May be there-and-back or multiple exit.
Diptych. Neditor creates two maps that link together in some manner.


That's all for now, folks!

#16678 1 2007-08-11T13:13:31Z gloomp
I am a member of a photo website called flickr, and on it my main interest is group called 365 days (yes, days should be capitalized, it's my thing not to). 365 days is a project where you take one self portrait every day. The goal is to do it for one year (after that it's optional). Currently, there are 4913 members to the group, and while not all of them do the project, there is certainly more that one thousand people doing it. I, of course, am one of them. I have been doing the project since new years' day, or 222 days.
However, for the founder of the group, ChrisMaverick, and a few other people, today is day 365, so it seemed appropriate to create the topic today.

Feel you might find this at least mildly interesting, so discuss.

#17537 1 2007-10-13T21:23:48Z gloomp
So waaaaay back there was a game called "The Make a Map Game."
I think it was a cool idea, but it sort of degraded, so I'm starting it up again, but with a few changes.

Original Outline:
Start with a blank map. First person adds two objects, second person adds two objects, etc.

New Outline
Start with a blank map. First person adds FIVE objects OR tiles, second person adds FIVE objects OR tiles, etc.

Original rules:
You may not add tiles.
You may move some objects around.
We'll decide when the map is finished as a group.

Updated rules:
You may add five tiles, five objects, or any combination of each.
You may not move any objects around.
We'll decide when the map is finished as a group.

Thats the basic idea.
Have fun!
data attached


Oh, and PLEASE don't turn the map into a DDA!

#372570 3 2007-10-17T14:06:26Z gloomp
Artist Commentary of the Third Kind by gloomp

Mr. LeWitt's All-Gold Challenge: I REALLY love modern and contemporary art, that's something you should know about me, and so I took notice when a few months ago Sol LeWitt, a talented artist who helped launch minimalism, died.
I made this map with no thought of LeWitt, but when it came time to submit it to NUMA, I decided to dedicate it to him due to the maps simplicity and hard, geometric feel.
Contrary too popular belief, there is more than one way to get back through the bounceblocks.

Rain in the Afternoon: I just love this. Plain and simple, might be the best map I've ever made.

Inspired by a Story, a Story of Infiltration: I really pulled out all the stops on this one. It's incredibly long and complex. It really might be too big, I've never quite decided.

. . . Stupid Hare: This is a good example of what good object placement can do to a bland tileset. The tileset is just a few clusters of 'E' tiles and the Metanet logo, not interesting. But chain those 'E's together, add some gold in the resultant chimneys, and stick thwumps at the bottom 'em, and you've got some mighty exciting gameplay!
And the title's another art reference, this time a recent Christopher Wool (Google him) painting.

Head of State: When you look at this, it looks like pointless clutter, when you play it, you discover what's cool about it.
I worked hard on the style of this, and thought over every object. I tried to fit subtle images of conformity and rebellion into it, such as the line of straight, perfect gold leading to death.

Matchplay: Preview of a map pack that I'll start releasing shortly.
I recognized that the tiles in this level were going overwhelm any attempts at maximalist object placement, so I tried to keep it simple, basic, an action map stripped down to the bare bones.
I think I pulled it off nicely, although the exit switch area could be a lot nicer.

Shekerboard: 03-3 in my map pack "A Beginning."
Why did I choose to include this one? Because it's so damn fun. I swear, there is not much that's more exhilarating and fun in N than rushing down a gauntlet.

Beyond the Call: From my Vehemence maps.
I remember this map taking a LOT longer than my other Vehemence maps.
The tileset is basically cleaned up scribbles on a spiral template.
It's mines that took a while, because with mine-jumpers, you need to playtest a lot.
I guess you could say it's 20mm-jumper.

In Aqutite: I didn't really know what I was gonna do when I started this map, just that I wanted to make an interesting and unusual map based off a room-like tileset.
When starting, I didn't really intend to make such a restrained map, it just sorta happened.
The thwump room really seems to clash with a lot of the map, but I like it, it sorta balances out the rest of the map. In fact, now that I look at it, all the rooms seem sorta full.
Oh well. Still works.

Somewhat Hard: I've decided to finish this off with my first map.
This remarkably managed to get a public rating of 3/5.
I think today that it's quite dull, especially with the launchpad, but back then I found it exciting and hard.
He he. Times change, huh?

---

I might have made a few gramatical errors, sorry if I did.
This was a lot of fun to do and I strongly encourage other mappers to do it.

#373720 6 2007-10-22T16:09:32Z gloomp
Okay, time for level-by-level commentary.

Underneath the block. This is okay for the first level, but I really would have liked a gauss or something by the exit switch. 3/5

Use your weight. Biggest problem: the gold was very hard to get (for me at least.) the gold, largely, should not be that hard to get this early. So while I found it a fun challenge, it does detract from your score.
Other than that, however, it's well-executed and fun map based off an idea that's been used a million times. 3/5

n-maze. I had fun playing this. It's good for where it is.
The name has been used a million times, though. I'd recommend, off the top of my head, "Irrelevant Security."
The gameplay is fine, a bit bland, but that's expected.
The aesthetics could use work, also. Perhaps make cool shapes or patterns in the tiles. 3.5/5

Underground 1. Boy, you need work on your names. Try "Underground 1: Smash Pack", or something like that.
Some major problems:
1) The mines are spaced too close together. You only need half of them.
2) The drone does nothing.
3) I generally would avoid placing mines in corners, but no biggie. 2.5/5

Death Bridges. As you'll see in the demos, this is very cheatable. And you've put the mines to closely together again.
I can see what you were going for but it just didn't work out. 2.5/5

--

Whew, hope that helps. Sorry if I was harsh, but it's my opinion, and I tried to deliver it constructively.
Also, my inspiration to came from here.
Here's demos. put them, one by one, into the replay box in userlevels.

I've found that if you repeatedly hold your breath for as long as you can over the course of at least half an hour, taking time in between each attempt to let your breath return to normal, you can eventually enter a Zen-like state and hold your breath much longer.
This works especially well if you have warm water to float on.

#381835 3 2007-11-22T13:42:13Z gloomp
Well, anything by Nancy Kress, for starters, but I'd also recommend:


The Wonderful O, by James Thurber. Delightful little fable. The version I read was wonderfully illustrated.

If you want something a little heavier, i am no one you know (yes, uncapitalized,) by Joyce Carol Oates. For me, the crowning glory of this collection filled to the gullet with gems, would be Aiding and Abetting, a tension-filled, ferocious little story. The book is not perfect, though. Steer clear of Me & Wolfie, 1979, an indecipherable mess of a story.

For something between the two extremes, try Life of Pi, by Yann Martel. You may find it a little slow to start, but you soon grow to enjoy the long, winding narrative, told by a fascinating narrator, who follows three different religions.


If I think of more I'll post 'em.

#18536 1 2007-12-23T15:05:04Z gloomp
So, I saw Cossack's post in the Ron Mueck thread. I totally disagree.

He used two very visible examples of modern art: Marcel Duchamp's "Fountain," and Damien Hirst's "the Physical Impossibility of Death in the Mind of Someone Living." While these are both excellent examples of modern art, it is a misconception that these works were created with arrogance.

When Duchamp signed a urinal and declared it art, he was trying to test the word of the organizers of an exhibition that claimed they [paraphrasing] "would exhibit all work submitted." [/paraphrasing]
Keep in mind as well that he was a member of Dada, so he was seeking to create "anti-art."

When Hirst made P.I.D.M.S.L, he was trying to make a lasting impression. He wanted the viewer to feel something, and judging by the controversy, it worked.
Last summer I had the honor of seeing it in person, and let me tell you, do not judge it until you've seen it in person your self; it holds an awesome weight.
(as a side note, Cossack referred to P.I.D.M.S.L as a "a shark cut in half," which is inaccurate.)


I've opened this thread for a discussion of modern art, so feel free to expand the scope beyond what I've covered.
#392690 4 2007-12-23T15:30:11Z gloomp

#392807 5 2007-12-23T23:53:57Z gloomp
Quote: Myrrhman, 2007-12-23T22:56:32Z
I'm not too up to snuff on this decade, but the art world since the 70's has become increasingly pluralistic. A new style is born every year and it seems to always follow the art world dialogue (more like tug of war) between two polars (vaguely) : tradition and modernism (realism and conceptualism; formalism and expressionism; there are plenty of terms.)

I mostly agree here. It certainly seems that a new style is developed every year, that the art world has a movement of the week, then moves on. I'm not sure, though, that tug of war is entirely accurate. During the Photorealist period, and especially in the works of Malcolm Morely, realism and conceptualism became one.
Quote: Myrrhman, 2007-12-23T22:56:32Z
It is a shame that art sort of ate itself in so much as the fact you have to learn to really appreciate it. That you may need a pamphlet to understand a piece is entirely the art world's fault.

I think the best art is interesting to look at, and equally interesting to read about.
Quote: Myrrhman, 2007-12-23T22:56:32Z
That being said, I wish more people would explore. There's a lot out there besides du Champ (the "new" father of modern art) and Hirst (who I never particularly liked.)

Totally. Look beyond Duchamp, and you find Dada, in which you find Max Ernst, who was a member of the Surrealists, to whom Joseph Cornell has been linked. He's also been linked to Pop art, and so on and so forth.
I liked Hirst's early stuff, but his most recent pieces a just stupid.
Quote: Myrrhman, 2007-12-23T22:56:32Z
By the way, gloomp, do you study art at all or just have a passing interest?

I got interested when I was ten, was subscribed to Art in America by eleven, made thousands and thousands of drawings, photographs and the like, and amassed a large amount of art books, and by now probably have a passable history of 20th century art in my head, all during my free time.


Ad: is more recent work is much more interesting to you philistines.

iangb: the art world is more massively corrupt that anyone not in it can imagine, I think.

#393262 17 2007-12-25T16:36:12Z gloomp
Quote: Goatman, December 24, 2007 09:18 pm
Quote: Cossack, December 25, 2007 01:38 am
. a bunch of manakins lying on the floor with red pain over them. there were a tonn of other crap like it. its just not art. did the person try hard, learn for years and produce that with effort. no, he got a bunch of manakins, dropped them to the floor and then went and threw red paint over it. i could do it too, but instead when i was into art properly i went for the more conventional style of actual art as opposed to gimmics.
Yes, but you are forgetting two things.

Firstly, the piece you were looking at was most likely conceptual art. And most conceptual artists would say that anyone can learn the skills of painting styles ,ie. the impressionists or Van Gough, but not everyone can work out how to put a statement, message, or feeling into their art, and hence conceptual is made to give the viewer an insight in to the artists opinions, not just skills.


Quote: Cossack, 2007-12-25T01:38:06Z
thats an empty room with lights in it. its a waste of space. it inspires no feeling other than surprise to why someone actually let him use that space. its not art - i say again - its degeneration.

Secondly, about the above. Chances are Martin Creed would be rather happy that you are challenging his piece. As I said above, conceptual I believe is made to express an artists opinion. And so Martin Creed is trying to inspire a feeling of being used (paying to get into a gallery to see just bare walls), outrage (as you are obviously showing now), and inspiring critique. Also, to me this work is challenging the thought of where the artwork ends and the gallery begins; if something's in a gallery, does that make it art? And so if a gallery is within a gallery, is that gallery art?

And there is almost always a meaning behind an artwork. In fact, I have a challenge for you Cossack. Find a piece of art with absolutely no meaning. If you can, my argument will be smashed into a million pieces. But if you can't, yours will.
Exactly what I would say, only said better.

Quote: wedgie, 2007-12-26T02:18:46Z
Reading this reminds me of the 'guerilla' graffiti artist 'Banksy.' Banksy is known as a graffiti artist/prankster that creates some incredible public artwork that have some really clever undertones and meanings.
One prank of Banksy's entailed hanging his own drawing of a caveman pushing a shopping trolley on a piece of rock in a British museum that remained unnoticed for 2 days.
He has also made his own artwork and hung them in several major galleries in America, where they have also taken a while to realize that they were fakes.
Ah yes, Banksy. I flat out love this work. Period.

Umm . . . they are real works of art with real intentions, real ideas, real thought. They're just funny.
Quote: wedgie, 2007-12-26T02:18:46Z
This is not necessarily a dig at modern art, it's more of a look at art galleries and the way that they choose what should go in them.

No argument here. Major art galleries (Gagosian is the most obvious example) will often show work by once talented artists that, to be blunt, is atrocious.
Quote: wedgie, 2007-12-26T02:18:46Z
While I am here though, one thing that annoys me a little about some modern art is this whole 'making the viewer feel something by looking at their work' idea. To create controversy or to see what different emotions people show from looking at the work. I mean anyone can just get something and call it art, and whatever they get is bound to cause some sort of reaction because that is just human nature.

Yes, but which is better, a pretty landscape painting of rolling hills and valleys by visually talented painter that you'll forget about in a few days, or a room with walls covered in mirrors and floor covered in a few inches of crystal water filled with tiny lights hanging from the ceiling and reflecting endlessly that you'll remember for years (and trust me, you will)?
Quote: wedgie, 2007-12-26T02:18:46Z
What I mean is that it's hard to not get some sort of reaction from someone when they see something. If you just see some random object that is being called art then you are bound to start thinking 'what the hell is this?' Then this reaction means that the author has succeeded in making the viewer feel something. Well done genius. I bet that took you a long time to think that up. Creating something that you know is going to cause a particular outcome , then saying that said outcome is what you are 'going for' in this piece. 'Yes I am going to sign a urinal and put it on display as I want the public to feel different emotions when they see it.' Well of course they are going to feel different emotions, it's just a fecking urinal! [/rant]

But . . . but . . . okay, lemme spell it out for you.
Duchamp was not thinking about the thoughts of the viewer when he signed the urinal. HE WAS TRYING TO REBEL AGAINST THE ART ESTABLISHMENT BY PUTTING MEMBERS OF IT IN A BIND. IF THEY REFUSED TO ACCEPT IT, SOMETHING A WELL ESTABLISHED ARTIST WAS CALLING ART, THAN THEY WERE GOING BACK ON THEIR WORD. IF, HOWEVER, THEY ACCEPTED IT, THEY WOULD BE ACCEPTING SOMETHING THAT WAS NOT ART, WHICH WOULD DEFEAT THE WHOLE PURPOSE.
I do not, repeat, not think that a urinal is art (accept for those by Robert Gober.)
Plus, there is so. Much. More to modern art than that one f-cking urinal. It makes me come that close to swearing whenever anyone say think that Readymades are all there is to modern art. I challenge you to find one art exhibition that has included a Readymade made after 1970.
Quote: wedgie, 2007-12-26T02:18:46Z
Anyway, I love art. I have gone up to A-levels with it. I love sketching and painting, in particular, photo realistic style. But some modern art just really gets on my nerves. I understand what they are doing, or at least trying to do, but I just think that a monkey could do the same thing and get the same reaction.

Just because a monkey could do it doesn't mean it would do it. Big difference.
Quote: wedgie, 2007-12-26T02:18:46Z
P.p.s. Just thought I'd show this while I am here too. This is a very early AS level self portrait. Me

Quite nice. Very well done. Much better that I could do.

#18706 1 2008-01-04T23:39:25Z gloomp
The Linear Map Competition


I believe that linear maps are vastly under appreciated due to their being used most by n00bs.

Linear maps can be great in the right hands (i.e. Cerberus,) but too few talented authors use them often enough.

Therefore, I'm starting this competition.


Linear Maps, by this Competition's Standards:
  • ONLY one path, not including for gold. Gold may be placed slightly off-path.
  • Path is always clear
  • Slight doubling back is acceptable, but try to keep it minimal, unless it's a there-and-back
Hm. Thought there was more
Rules not Regarding Map Style:
  • Competitors may submit up to five maps
  • Maps may be submitted as a .txt in this thread or on NUMA
  • An APG (All Possible Gold) Demo must be Submitted with the map
  • Map(s) must be submitted by February 1st
  • If submitted to NUMA, include tags "Linear" and "LMC"
Judging:
I will need four more judges (assuming this takes off) as well as myself to judge on these factors:
  • Fun. /50
  • Aesthetics (sorry Twistkill!). /25
  • Creativity. /25
For a total out of one hundred.

THE SINGLE MOST IMPORTANT RULE:
  • Have fun!


Judges:
gloomp
Guy_Zap
mam

Judging
gloomp:

Insomniac Awakening by Izzy

Fun: 26. Was a little frustrating at times with the lasers.
Aesthetics: 17. Resembles a Metanet map, and feels solid, but vertical tile shape threw off the balance.
Creativity: 12. All been done before, but fits together nicely.


55


Hugging a Vine of Poison Ivy by Sneekman

Fun: 15. Sorry, but I just found it way to hard.
Aesthetics: 15. The mines were REALLY ugly, but the tileset was good.
Creativity: 16. Been done countless times, but there aren't that many on this level of difficulty

46


Passing the Guards by the_apprentice

Fun: 30. The rockets were too hard, but overall it was a good tunnel map.
Aesthetics. 15. Nice tileset, but some of the objects looked really bad.
Creativity: 15. It has some atmosphere.

60


Clay Hologram by Goatfeet

Fun: 40. It was challenging, but not frustrating. In fact, it made me rather more calm.
Aesthetics: 10. The tiles do their duty.
Creativity: 22. It's not easy making a good tile and jump only map.

72


Floor 222 by Goatsneek and Feetman

Fun: 35. It's easy, relaxed and enjoyable, but a little awkward at times.
Aesthetics: 18. Haphazard, but somehow works.
Creativity: 15. Gold is original, but it's mostly standard.

68

#414359 2 2008-03-06T14:15:04Z gloomp
I have no idea why, but I've taken it upon myself to review each and every one of these:


There's no law against putting pennies in my ears, SUPERSTOOP!

If the gold here was any less interesting, this would be a failure. As is, it's a good map. Because you go for the gold, that gives the drones time to fall into their pattern.

Knights of Cydonia

Starts off nice, but after you get the switch, it all goes to Hell in a handbasket. Either I was following the totally wrong path, or the flow really was that stupid. I kept not collecting gold, redoing jumps, and never actually feeling like I was in a race. Plus, the seekers don't sense you.

Three Laps

So, you need to go around three times, right? Really, that's all? Sounds fun! Huh, wuzzat? It's a drone race?! That's stupid!!!

Temple to Frustraton

Ignoring the typo, the name's fairly accurate. The biggest problem is how tacked on the bottom is; it feels totally different than the rest of the map. The best part is definitely the gold design, so I'm gonna have to steal it.

Gigachip

This would be a lot better if you removed the outside thwumps, closed off all the side openings, and added an entrance at the top. THEN it would be fun.

Ticker Tape

Now this is fun. Everything works together wonderfully, as though engaged in some fantastical dance.

Potatoheart

I have mixed feelings about this one. I liked how you used one-ways and I liked the cache of gold at the top, but I didn't like the start. Overall, it's decent, but no classic.

#439125 34 2008-05-25T21:27:44Z gloomp
Okay, I've gotten better acquainted with newma, and I feel I should list the problems I have with it.
  • I don't like how there's only one featured map per day. In time it might become more interesting, but I really think it would be better if the title were split between those that were added that day.
  • The tag space is much too small!
  • As I said before, I hate that the point ratings have been removed.
  • I don't like that the name edit bug has been fixed. I think if it's changed back to the way it was, than we wouldn't get more spam names. Rather, I think, we'd get more long good ones, like KA's.
But don't get me wrong, I love it, I just have a few problems with it.

#20453 1 2008-06-06T17:03:29Z gloomp
The Linear Map Competition Two


I believe that linear maps are vastly under appreciated due to their being used most by n00bs.

Linear maps can be great in the right hands (i.e. Domination, spuzalot), but too few talented authors use them often enough.

Therefore, I'm starting this competition.

Yup, it's back, and it's better than ever! Now, to paste a large amount of the first thread, with some changes.
Quote
Linear Maps, by this Competition's Standards:
  • ONLY one path, not including for gold. Gold may be placed slightly off-path.
  • Path is always clear
  • Slight doubling back is acceptable.
Hm. Thought there was more
Rules not Regarding Map Style:
  • Competitors may submit one map per round. They may withdraw and submit a new map if the first has not yet been judged.
  • Maps may be submitted as a .txt in this thread or on NUMA.
  • An APG (All Possible Gold) demo must be submitted with the map.
  • If submitted to NUMA, include tags "Linear" and "LMC"
Judging:
I will need two more judges (assuming this takes off) as well as myself plus a back-up to judge on these factors:
  • Fun. /50
  • Aesthetics (sorry Twistkill!). /25
  • Use of Theme. /25
For a total out of one hundred. Judges should submit their judging at the end of the week, in a chunk. It is recommended to compile it throughout the week, though.
THE SINGLE MOST IMPORTANT RULE:
  • Have fun!

Noticed the differences yet? I'll explain.

LMC2 is going to be split into rounds, and each round will have a theme. Be forewarned, these will be weird themes, and I encourage you to be creative with them.
After the deadline (a week) is up, judging will be tallied, and if you have the lowest score, you're out.
Wondering about prizes? So am I. I'm open to any weird and wacky suggestions you have.

Participants
kisler - http://nmaps.net/127332
SilverFender - http://nmaps.net/127128 - http://nmaps.net/127836 - http://nmaps.net/128729 - http://nmaps.net/129229
DDRave - http://nmaps.net/127165 - http://nmaps.net/128167 - http://nmaps.net/128976
Sonic-Fan101 - http://nmaps.net/127242 - http://nmaps.net/128420
_destiny^- - http://nmaps.net/127123 - http://nmaps.net/127821
BEASTLYBOY - http://nmaps.net/127407
Riobe - http://nmaps.net/127168 - http://nmaps.net/128744 - http://nmaps.net/128744 - http://nmaps.net/129248 - http://nmaps.net/129698 - http://nmaps.net/129889
Nexx
notkitt - http://nmaps.net/127425 - http://metanet.2.forumer.com/index.php?act...=post&id=481505 - http://metanet.2.forumer.com/index.php?act...=post&id=484395 - http://metanet.2.forumer.com/index.php?act...=post&id=484884

#442488 5 2008-06-06T19:29:42Z gloomp
Quote: kisler, June 06, 2008 03:17 pm
Define 'linear.'

How can you rate for aesthetics on a linear map?
Have a look through the first thread.


Oh, and
Quote
linear [lin-ee-er]
adjective
1. of, consisting of, or using lines: linear design.
2. pertaining to or represented by lines: linear dimensions.
3. extended or arranged in a line: a linear series.
4. involving measurement in one dimension only; pertaining to length: linear measure.
5. of or pertaining to the characteristics of a work of art in which forms and rhythms are defined chiefly in terms of line.
6. having the form of or resembling a line: linear nebulae.
7. Mathematics.
a. consisting of, involving, or describable by terms of the first degree.
b. having the same effect on a sum as on each of the summands: a linear operation.
8. Electronics. delivering an output that is directly proportional to the input: a linear circuit; a linear amplifier.
9. threadlike; narrow and elongated: a linear leaf.
[Origin: 163545; < L līneāris of, belonging to lines. See line1, -ar1]

Related forms
linearly, adverb

#444624 10 2008-06-15T00:52:36Z gloomp
Quote: incluye, June 14, 2008 07:21 pm
It's like the widespread teaching of, oh, such "icons" as the evolutionary tree, Haeckel's embryos, Java Man, and the Miller/Urey as accurate facts.
I'll ignore everything else, and just focus on that.
Haeckel's embryos are not, as many Creationists believe, included as fact in evolutionary textbooks anymore. I know this thanks to the film Pack of Dodos. In that film, a documentary, the filmmaker goes to the home of a prominent Creationist and interviews him. In the interview, the Creationist say that modern textbooks still claim Haeckel's embryos as fact. The filmmaker ask him to prove this, so he and the filmmaker go to his shelf of textbooks and look for any of the books representing Haeckel's embryos as fact. After a long search, the Creationist finds one - published in 1913. He finds no later ones.

Just a quick other note - Intelligent Design doesn't really hold water for me because the some animals designs are really redundant, pointless, and stupid.

EDIT: Oh, to answer the question: I can believe there might be some supernatural force in the world, but it's not that important to me.

#463574 316 2008-08-22T13:50:30Z gloomp
THE ADVENTURES OF BACKWARDS A + FRONTWARDS A

BACKWARDS A was rather ill last night as we pulled up to camp. Six days into the bronchial flu-bug that has dogged BACKWARDS A since the last day of our Baikal tour.

BACKWARDS A took BACKWARDS A into the tent to lie down and soon started feeling unnaturally warm not entirely unpleasantly so after a chilly afternoon on the camels. However after dinner it was obvious that BACKWARDS A had a touch of fever. After a conference with FRONTWARDS A and Baigal (who is a trainee doctor when shes not a tour guide) we all agreed that BACKWARDS A wasnt getting any better so BACKWARDS A caved in and started on the five tablet course of our emergency Rulide antibiotics. BACKWARDS A then took BACKWARDS A back to bed.

Around ten oclock after coughing continuously for another two hours BACKWARDS A brain registered how hideously overheated BACKWARDS A had become and BACKWARDS A ripped off half BACKWARDS A clothes, blew BACKWARDS A nose, crawled back into BACKWARDS A bag and spent the rest of the night in fitful, but much improved sleep.

When we awoke BACKWARDS A was feeling much better perhaps the Rulide had already begun to take effect but where BACKWARDS A had ridden BACKWARDS A camel yesterday, at the base of my pelvis where it meets the top of the back of my thigh, BACKWARDS A had two large raised welts such as youd get on your skull if someone tried to frag you with a baseball bat. Sitting hurt like mad and it didnt bode too well for the second day of camel-riding that lay ahead of us, let alone the hypothetical days three and four.

With a pretty uncertain heart BACKWARDS A helped FRONTWARDS A pack up our stuff and our tent (still proving to be thoroughly awesome). After Mugi had watered the camels with Gambas help and the rest of the campsite had been lifted we remounted Stinky, Judge Dredd and Sir Grumpy Fartsalot and set out to the west.

The morning was excruciating. Mugis camel (christened Sir Fartsalot by us for reasons that should be fairly clear) was restless and kept stopping to eat, with our two older specimens following suit, meaning we were going along at about two or three kilometres per hour, at barely a fraction of human walking pace. After the first hour BACKWARDS A posterior was on fire with constant pain, as BACKWARDS A greater than average-Mongolian height meant BACKWARDS A had to sit the hard edge of the scanty felt saddle. Coupled with this was the acute pain in BACKWARDS A knees and calves deriving from the insufficient length of BACKWARDS A already maximised stirrup-straps.

Our presumed lunchtime about one oclock rolled around with no sign of the jeep, and by twenty past two when we finally made our rendezvous BACKWARDS A doubts, pains and a generic wellspring of toxic emotion had reached some kind of internal waterhead.

The tour to date has been a little confused, with many alterations to the itinerary made in advance without our knowledge, and further changes occurring day to day, as Khongor has never run this particular tour before. Some items, such as travelling from Bainsag to the Great Sand Dune and back by camel, are simply impossible, as the round trip would take close to a fortnight! BACKWARDS A was feeling grumpy about the consequent relative pointlessness of our camel-trekking, as we wouldnt reach any destinations of note in the saddle.

FRONTWARDS A and BACKWARDS A discussed things and she told me to make the call as BACKWARDS A was the sick one and grumpier about camel-pain, so we arranged with Baigal, Gamba and Mugi to complete the visits to the remaining important sites by jeep starting the following day, and to abandon the camel trek from the end of the day. The whole thing made BACKWARDS A feel like a bit of a complaining heel, but BACKWARDS A relief was also palpable. BACKWARDS A not really well enough for this.

Afternoon on Judge Dredd, while still infernally painful, was somehow far preferable to the morning session. After a heavy bout of lunchtime feeding the camels hit their stride, to the point that BACKWARDS A wasnt sure BACKWARDS A couldve matched them on foot. This led to a doubly increased variety of sights in terrain that was already more varied than in the morning, including rocky slopes, rapidly desiccating salt-marshes, and saxaul-studded desert with some seriously large sand dunes. Along the way we stopped for salty ricy tea wth a bemused nomad family, saw a desert fox fleeing modestly over a nearby sandhill, and witnessed a half-hearted crane mating display, perhaps so lacklustre because it looked like there was at most one dating alternative in sight!

We also experienced our first running on the camels, as our steeds topped ten kilometres per hour in a juddering gait that at certain frequencies was far less painful than the rolling walk, or at least a welcome novel type of pain.

On a long, wide tableland facing a chain of mountains we intersected again with the jeep, and rocklike Mugi almost immediately began his return to Bainsag with all three camels, although he was still planning to do the trip over the next two days. We wobbled down to a lower altitude in the jeep and pitched our tents on some spongy dried mud amidst tufts of dead foliage from which we would build a fire. BACKWARDS A was feeling much less sick, and much less ill-humoured, than at the same time on the previous day, and dinner was stacked with garlic.
